tbmm-2020-02-19sit01spe0002par0014	Thank you, Mr. President. Mr. President, dear lawmakers, because of February 21st, World Mother Language Day, I have taken an off-the-book promise, and I salute you all. In 2000, the United Nations Organisation for Education, Science and Culture UNESCO accepted February 21st as World Language Day. For 20 years, UNESCO has organised a series of events on February 21st to create linguistic awareness and encourage multilingualism in the world, celebrating World Mother Language Day. UNESCO celebrates World Mother Language Day with a different theme every year. For example, last year the World Mother Language Day was celebrated with the theme of the native languages of the world, and this year there will be celebrations in the theme of languages that do not know them. UNESCO rightly sees multilingual and linguistic diversity as indispensable to sustainable development and lasting peace. See, 40 percent of the languages spoken around the world are in danger of extinction. More than 7,000 languages are spoken, and 40 per cent of this is supposed to disappear within the next hundred years. The website, Etnology, speaks of 39 languages spoken in Turkey. According to UNESCO World Danger Languages Atlas, earlier the name of this Atlas was the Red Book of Languages Under Earth -- 18 languages have been destroyed or in danger of extinction in Turkey, friends. Three of them are completely destroyed, the Greek of Ubih, Mlahso, and Cappadocia, and the language of Hertevin faces extinction. Between Hertevin and Mlahso, Suryanian languages. Turoyo, another Suranian language, is also seen at risk on the UNESCO list. UNESCO makes a distinction regarding endangered languages. First, the most dangerous languages spoken in our country: Hertevin, in this category, is one of the Northeast languages of Siirt origin. According to 1999 data, there are only a thousand people who speak this language. There are languages in our country that are seriously in danger: Gagavuz, Ladino spoken by Turkish Jews, and Turoyo, as I just said, the Suryani language. There are definitely endangered languages: Abazaca, Çishina, Lazca, Pontus, Gypsy languages, "Atlas" in the Romanice-Suryani family; and, according to Atlas, there is no one in Turkey who speaks this language anymore, and the last ones who spoke it left the country. The unsecured languages are Abhazca, Adi Night, Cabar-Cerca, and Zazaca. UNESCO has made a series of initiatives to protect endangered languages in many countries, but it has no programs in Turkey. I think there's only one reason: either Turkey feels that it does not have any concern about the dying languages, or Turkey thinks it cannot run such a program. UNESCO publishes a guide along with Atlas. This manual also says how a language can be protected from extinction. Now I'm going to read a few sentences from this manual. He says that the most important thing that can be done to protect a language from extinction is to create the proper conditions so that people can speak it and teach it to their children. It is often necessary for national policies, which recognise and protect minority languages, education systems that support native language education, to create a creative writing system and a formal structure between the language-speaking community and the linguistics. The most decisive factor is to create a social and political environment that encourages multilingualism and respect for minority languages, not to create a language that makes it feel rich, but to protect languages that are in danger, according to UNESCO. During the period of your government, different policies were implemented on different languages and dialects at different times. The protection, development, learning of the native language and education in the native language have often been perceived as a security issue in this country, not as a basic human right. I don't want to accuse the Justice and Development Party governments alone, because that policy was being evaluated by a security look during the previous administrations. But I'll tell you with a few examples that there's been an attack on the Kurdish language recently. Look, you've only closed many institutions with the State of Emergency.

Only 1% of teenage girls exposed to sexual violence received professional help. According to TÜİK data, 18,623 children were sexually abused in our country in 2017. What is being done to prevent sexual abuse? UNICEF is proposing that education, social welfare, justice and health systems, which prevent child abuse, should be programmed to cooperate with society and children. Social cultural norms that will excuse violence with economic, social inequality that will change adult behavior should be corrected, under-reviewed, taught that violence can and can be identified. In Article 41 of our Constitution, the government takes protective measures against abuse and violence. Likewise, in Article 103 of our penal code, there can be no less than 18 years of punishment if the victim has not completed the age of twelve if the child is sexually abused by the child for eight to fifteen years, no less than sixteen years in case an organ or sair object is placed in the body of the child. tbmm-2020-02-19sit03spe0163par0022	Mr. President, lawmakers have been described as shortcomings in the banking system as the basis for most economic problems that occur worldwide and cause problems on a global scale. The inevitable result of the globalized world economic system has increased capital activity, while banks have moved away from the classical banking structure and the operational risk definitions have changed. At this point, there is a general consensus that measures must be taken before problems arise. As you know, as banking activities grow, the risk areas are increasing. The global economic system is trying to establish global regulations against continuous expanding credit volumes and the risks of diversified financial instruments. In 1988, the Basel Banking Control Committee was established within the International Exchange Bank. The first criteria known as Basel 1 have been published and further, with new problems emerging, the benchmarks Basel 2 and Basel 3 have been announced. As you can see, global scale regulations are being made based on the experience of developing economic systems and increasing risks. At the same time, the amendments to some of the articles of Banking Law 5411 are important in preventing emerging markets and risks. Members of parliament, important changes are being made, such as redesigning the definition of the banks' RSC group, expanding the volume of loans, expanding the funding resources of development and investment banks, drafting a precautionary plan before the financial problems of banks, organising transactions that lead to financial stability, taking into account the differences of development and investment banks and accession banks. As it is known, in Turkey, accession banks are essentially competing with deposit banks in similar conditions. The fact that even the non-interest banking system has serious differences in itself is subject to similar restrictions with commercial banks prevents the development of this alternative source of funds. The change in the single-handed determination of the commission, wages and costs of the banks concerned with our citizens will also increase the trust of the depositors in the banking system and in the banking system. It is also thought that the upgrade of fines to the banking system in exchange for transactions that will eliminate trust would be useful in reducing the risks that will arise in the system. With my influence, the changes that have been made are aimed at reducing the risks of the banking system that may and may come out in our country, while on the other hand, the system is allowed to expand credits.
